Households deleverage – governments do not
The global household-debt ratio fell to 60.9% of GDP
in 2025, almost 10pps below its 2005 level and nearly
13pps below its 2009 peak (Figure 21). This does not
mean that households stopped borrowing: liabilities
continued to increase, but more slowly than nominal
economic output. Nevertheless, the contrast with the
public sector is striking. While government debt has
continued to rise, households have spent much of the

past decade strengthening their balance sheets.
But deleveraging was far from universal. Since 2005, the
debt ratio has fallen by 20.4pps in North America and
by 6.3pps in Western Europe, while Japan’s ratio has
remained broadly stable. Australia and New Zealand
stand apart from this trend: their combined ratio rose by
12.2pps to 113.8%, although it remains below its 2020
peak. The decline in the global ratio therefore conceals
a significant shift in the geography of household debt,

from the traditional advanced economies towards
emerging markets, albeit with important exceptions.
China recorded by far the largest increase. Its
household-debt ratio rose by 42.1pps to 59.4% of GDP
between 2005 and 2025, reflecting the extraordinary
credit and property boom of the intervening years.
However, the ratio has barely changed since 2020,
underlining how abruptly that boom has ended. China’s
household-debt ratio is now only around 10pps below

that of the US, but the two countries have followed
opposite paths: American households have deleveraged
substantially, while China has moved from rapid debt
accumulation to balance-sheet caution.
Elsewhere in the emerging world, the increase was less
dramatic. Since 2005, the household-debt ratio has
risen by 10.7pps in Latin America to 27.8% and by 6.1pps
in Eastern Europe to 21.8%. Both remain moderate by
international standards. Asia excluding Japan and China

also appears relatively stable, with its aggregate ratio
rising by only 2.2pps to 55.1%. But this regional average
is deceptive. It partly reflects the growing weight
of poorer, less-indebted economies and substantial
deleveraging in Singapore, where the ratio fell by
26.4pps to 51.6%.
At the country level, the fault lines cut across the
advanced and emerging-market divide. Switzerland and
Australia recorded debt ratios of 125.1% and 119.4% of
GDP , respectively, while Canada stood at 103.3% and

the Netherlands at 97.2%. Several Asian economies
also carry substantial household-debt burdens: Taiwan
and South Korea recorded ratios of around 92%, while
Thailand and Malaysia stood at approximately 87%
and 85%, respectively. These levels are comparable
with those recorded in several Western economies
before the global financial crisis. In 2006, household
debt stood at 98.4% of GDP in the US, 84.2% in Spain
and 95.0% in Ireland. High leverage does not in itself

signal an imminent crisis, but it makes households and
consumption more vulnerable to income, interest-rate
and housing-market shocks. The decisive dividing line is
therefore not between advanced and emerging markets,
but between housing and financial systems that rely
heavily on household leverage and those that do not.

Strong asset growth and subdued borrowing pushed

global net financial assets (financial assets minus
liabilities) up by +10.1% in 2025, broadly matching
the previous year’s +10.2%. They reached a record
EUR211.5trn, almost twice their 2015 level and more
than tripling their value in 2005.
China recorded the strongest increase in net financial
wealth at +15.3%, reflecting the combination of strong
asset growth and the near-standstill in household debt.
Latin America followed at +11.3%, while Eastern Europe

and Asia excluding China and Japan both recorded
growth of +11.0%. North America and Australia and
New Zealand also achieved double-digit increases
of +10.4% each. Japan recorded a strong +7.4%, while
Western Europe again brought up the rear at +5.2%.
Growth exceeded its 20-year average in almost every
region (Figure 22). Latin America was the only exception,
although its +11.3% increase was broadly in line with its
long-term rate.
The figures reveal a less visible source of wealth

creation. Weak credit growth may constrain housing
activity and household investment, but it also
strengthens private balance sheets when financial
assets continue to expand. In 2025, this deleveraging
dividend lifted global net financial wealth faster than
gross financial wealth. It also reinforced North America’s
dominant position: the region held 51.3% of global net
financial assets, compared with 48.1% of gross financial
assets. Low leverage relative to its enormous asset base

gives North America an additional wealth advantage.
Adjusted for population growth and inflation, China
remains the undisputed long-term leader (Figure 23).
Real net financial assets per capita have increased
more than sevenfold over the past two decades. No
other region comes close. Even the other emerging-
market regions recorded little more than a doubling.
Among advanced markets, Western Europe continued
to lag behind Japan, highlighting how weak asset

growth, rather than debt accumulation, has constrained
European wealth creation.
Comparing gross and net financial assets adds another
dimension. In China, net financial assets grew around
0.7pps more slowly per year than gross financial
assets over the past two decades, reflecting the rapid
accumulation of household debt. A smaller gap is
also visible in Eastern Europe. However, this is not a
universal emerging-market pattern: in Latin America
and the rest of Asia, gross and net wealth developed at

broadly similar rates. In the advanced economies, the
relationship is reversed. As household debt generally
grew more slowly than assets, net financial wealth
outpaced gross wealth. The difference was particularly
pronounced in North America, where net financial assets
grew around 0.7pps faster per year.

Examining the 25 richest countries by net financial assets
per capita at the end of 2025 reveals how significantly
debt can alter the ranking (Figure 24). Most notably,
the US ranks ahead of Switzerland to claim first place.
Although Switzerland remains ahead in gross financial
assets, its household liabilities of around EUR130,080
per capita are more than twice the US figure of
EUR52,730.
Taiwan and Sweden exchange fifth and sixth places.

Australia falls from eighth place in the gross ranking to
eleventh in the net ranking, while Ireland slips from 14th
to 15th, the UK from 15th to 18th and Norway from 12th
to 22nd. By contrast, Germany rises from 13th to 12th,
Italy from 16th to 13th, Japan from 18th to 14th and
Spain from 22nd to 21st. The Netherlands remains ninth
in both rankings. The comparison makes clear that gross
wealth tells only half the story: what ultimately matters
is how much remains after debt.

After a temporary acceleration to +4.7% in 2024,
growth in global real estate assets12 slowed again to
+3.5% in 2025. Apart from 2023, growth was weaker
only in the aftermath of the global financial crisis in
2012. Persistently high construction costs and elevated
mortgage rates continued to weigh on affordability,
transactions and new development. Overall, the value of
real estate assets in the countries covered by the report

reached EUR155.0trn (Figure 25)
Regional developments varied widely (Figure 26).
Eastern Europe recorded the strongest increase at
+10.6%, followed by Australia and New Zealand at
+8.5%. Both comfortably exceeded their long-term
growth rates despite the pressure from higher financing
costs. Japan also stood out: real-estate assets increased
by +5.0%, compared with a long-term average of
just +0.7%. Together with the recent acceleration in
household borrowing, this provides further evidence that

Japan may finally be leaving its long deflationary period
behind.
Western Europe recorded growth of +4.1%, slightly
above its long-term average of +3.5%. North America
meanwhile was the clear exception: growth slowed
sharply from +4.9% in 2024 to just +1.8% in 2025,
less than half its long-term rate of +3.9%. The global
slowdown was therefore driven primarily by the largest
housing market.

As with financial assets, a clearer picture emerges
when real-estate wealth is measured per capita and
adjusted for inflation (Figure 27). The long shadow of
Japan’s property crash remains visible: over the past two
decades, real-estate wealth per capita was essentially
flat in real terms, despite the recent recovery.
Real gains were positive in Australia and New Zealand,
North America and Western Europe, but generally
modest. Compared with financial assets, the gap was

particularly pronounced in North America, where real
financial assets per capita grew around 2pps faster
per year, and in Japan, where the gap was around
1.3pps. In Australia and New Zealand and Western
Europe, by contrast, the difference was only around
0.2pps annually. Property may provide stability, housing
services and diversification, but capital appreciation
alone has generally been a weaker engine of wealth
creation than participation in financial markets.

The country ranking of real estate assets nevertheless
shows how strongly housing can reshape the overall
picture. Switzerland had the highest real-estate assets
per capita at EUR350,450, followed by Australia
at EUR279,310 with the US taking the 7th spot at
EUR174,030. But what does the ranking look like when
net financial assets and real estate assets are presented
together? Figure 28 provides the answer. Switzerland
overtakes the US and claims first place, with combined

wealth of around EUR626,430 per capita. Australia
meanwhile is the biggest beneficiary, climbing seven
places from tenth in the adjusted net-financial-assets
ranking to third once property is included. Sweden
and Japan each fall six places, while Italy drops four.
Germany moves up one position to tenth. These shifts
expose two very different models of household wealth:
some countries build wealth predominantly through
financial markets, while others hold a much larger share

in bricks and mortar.

International: Catch-up loses momentum
For many years, global wealth development was
characterized by convergence, as emerging markets
gradually closed the gap with richer economies. That
process has not ended, but it has slowed dramatically.
The ratio of average net financial assets per capita in
advanced economies to those in emerging markets
fell from 62 in 2005 to 21 in 2015. This means that, on
average, the net financial assets of richer countries were

“only” 21 times higher than those of poorer countries in
2015. Over the following decade, however, it declined by
less than four points to 17 in 2025 (Figure 29).
Convergence reversed from 2017. After narrowing
rapidly for more than a decade, the wealth gap widened
again between 2017 and 2021. Convergence resumed
in 2022, but at a much slower pace: the ratio has fallen
from 18.5 to 17.2 in the past three years. The global
wealth gap is still closing, but has slowed to a crawl.

Declaring the end of globalization would be premature,
but its character has clearly changed. Geopolitical
tensions, weaker cross-border investment and the
reorganization of supply chains are eroding some of
the advantages that emerging markets derived from
ever-deeper global integration. At the same time, the
green transition and AI could create new opportunities
by increasing demand for critical resources, energy
infrastructure, data services and digitally delivered

work. Whether emerging economies can seize these
opportunities will depend on their access to technology,
capital and skills. For now, these transformations are
unfolding in a more fragmented world, creating a less
favorable environment for poorer countries to catch up.
Against this backdrop, the global wealth middle class
comprised around 866mn people in 2025.13 Although
its size changed little compared with the previous
year, its long-term expansion remains impressive:

membership has increased by 81% since 2005. More
importantly, its center of gravity has shifted. Emerging
markets accounted for 61.9% of its members in 2025,
up from 43.6% two decades earlier, with China alone
representing slightly more than half of the emerging-
market middle class.
Distribution:
New wealth, old divides

The transformation is even more striking among the
wealthy. The global high-wealth class grew by 37%

to around 584mn people between 2005 and 2025.
Emerging markets accounted for 32.1% of this group,
compared with less than 2% in 2005. This expansion
came entirely from emerging markets, while the number
of high-wealth individuals in advanced economies
declined slightly. China played the dominant role,
accounting for more than three-quarters of the
emerging-market total.
Emerging economies also generated most of the
growth in the global middle class. Taken together,

they accounted for more than 90% of the increase in
the middle and high-wealth classes over the past two
decades. The global wealth landscape has therefore
become far more diverse, even though the gap in
average wealth between advanced and emerging
markets remains wide.
The global low-wealth class grew by around 7% to
4.3bn people, while the population covered by the
analysis increased by almost 17%. Its share of the total
population consequently fell from 81.7% in 2005 to

74.8% in 2025. Emerging markets still accounted for
92.0% of this group, slightly below their 92.9% share two
decades earlier. However, the number of low-wealth
individuals in advanced economies increased by around
21% to 346mn. The global shift towards higher wealth
classes is therefore unmistakable, but it has by no means
benefited everyone.
The emergence of a larger and more geographically
diverse high-wealth class does not change the fact that

financial wealth remains extremely concentrated at the
global level. This becomes clear when the population of
the countries covered by the report is divided into deciles
according to net financial assets.
In 2025, the richest 10%, with average net financial
assets of approximately EUR312,590, owned 85.4% of
global net financial wealth. Their share has declined
from 91.5% in 2005, but remains far above the average
national top-decile share of 60.9% (Figure 30). At the

pace of improvement recorded over the past two
decades, it would take almost another eight decades
for global concentration to approach the level currently
observed within the countries.
13  The classification in wealth classes is based on worldwide average net financial assets per capita, which stood at EUR36,690 in 2025. The global
wealth middle class („middle wealth“, MW) includes all individuals with net financial assets of between 30% and 180% of the global average. This

means that for 2025, the rounded asset thresholds for the global wealth middle class are EUR11,000 and EUR66,000. The „low wealth“ (LW) catego -
ry, on the other hand, includes those individuals with net financial assets that are below a EUR11,000 threshold, while the term „high wealth“ (HW)
applies to those with net financial assets of more than EUR66,000 (for details on how the asset thresholds are set, see Appendix A).

### Page 38

Allianz Research
38
High global wealth concentration is also reflected in the
wide gap between median and average net financial
assets. In 2025, global median net financial wealth
stood at approximately EUR2,490 per capita, while the
average was almost 15 times as high at EUR36,690. The
gap, however, has narrowed substantially over time: in
2005, the average was almost 33 times the median. The
typical person has therefore gained ground relative to

the global average, but the average remains a poor
representation of ordinary wealth.
At first glance, the much faster growth of average net
wealth in most lower deciles appears encouraging
(Figure 31). However, these high growth rates largely
reflect extremely low starting points. Even large
percentage increases translate into modest absolute
gains, while the richest groups continue to accumulate
much larger sums. A more equal global distribution of

wealth therefore remains a distant prospect.

The gap between the richest decile and everyone else is

enormous. The second-richest decile owned just 8.7% of
global net financial assets, with an average of around
EUR34,060 per person. The richest tenth therefore held
nine times as much wealth per person as the group
immediately below it.
The bottom half of the population covered by the
report, comprising almost 3bn people, held virtually
nothing. Its combined share of global net financial
assets was approximately zero. The bottom decile had
negative net financial assets of around EUR2,680 per

person, as liabilities exceeded financial assets. Even
in the two deciles around the middle of the global
distribution, average net financial assets amounted to
only approximately EUR3,170 and EUR1,810 per person.
Global wealth has become more geographically diverse,
but it has hardly become broadly distributed.

National: More wealth, same divide
Net financial wealth is distributed less unevenly within
countries than across the world. In 2025, the richest 10%
owned an average of 60.9% of national net financial
assets across the 57 countries examined, using an
unweighted average, compared with 85.4% at the global
level. The gap between average and median wealth
was also considerably smaller: the national average-
to-median ratio was 3.12, again measured as an

unweighted average.
When it comes to inequality, however, the national
picture is particularly disappointing. Despite being a
major political issue for years, there has been virtually
no progress towards more even wealth distribution.
In 2005, the richest 10% held an average of 60.4% of
national wealth, while the average-to-median ratio
stood at 3.11. Two decades later, both measures are
almost unchanged. Wealth has grown substantially, but
its distribution within individual countries has remained

remarkably resistant to change.
This overall stability conceals sharper shifts at the
country level. In 32 of the 57 countries examined, the
share of the richest 10% changed by less than 2pps
between 2005 and 2025 (Figure 32). Where larger
shifts occurred, however, the balance was clearly tilted
towards greater inequality, with deterioration almost
twice as likely as improvement. Wealth concentration
increased in 16 countries and declined in only nine.

Among those recording an improvement, Cambodia
saw the largest decline in the top-decile share, followed
by Portugal and Slovakia. Latvia, Hungary, Belgium,
Ireland, Malaysia and the Netherlands also registered
meaningful decreases. Different starting points were at
work. Portugal, Latvia and Ireland moved down from
relatively high levels of concentration, while Slovakia,
Hungary and the Netherlands improved from already
more balanced positions. In the Netherlands, the richest

10% saw their share fall by 2.4pps to 56.1%.
At the other end of the spectrum, China recorded by far
the largest increase in concentration. The share of the
richest 10% rose by 15.7pps between 2005 and 2025 to
68.0%, well above the average national share of 60.9%.
This accompanied the enormous economic and social
transformation of the past two decades, which created
not only broad-based wealth but also a substantial
private upper class. China illustrates the difficulty of

combining rapid wealth creation with an equally rapid
diffusion of its benefits. After all, it was the unbridled
growth of the „wild“ 2000s, when the private sector
was subject to few restrictions, that primarily caused
inequality to rise. In sharp contrast, in the last five years,
the concentration of wealth has remained unchanged.

The ratio of average to median wealth provides an
important cross-check: it shows whether rising average
wealth is also reflected in the position of the typical

household. Across the 57 countries examined, the
average ratio remained virtually unchanged, rising from
3.11 in 2005 to 3.12 in 2025. This confirms the broader
picture of persistent national wealth inequality.
The strongest improvements occurred in the
Netherlands, where the ratio fell from 4.04 to 2.48, and
Ireland, where it declined from 4.87 to 3.42. In both
countries, the narrowing gap indicates that the middle
of the distribution gained ground relative to average

wealth.
The largest deteriorations were recorded in Russia,
Sweden and China. Russia’s ratio rose from 2.99 to 4.77,
while Sweden’s increased from 3.77 to 5.25. In China, it
climbed from 2.03 to 3.33. Median Chinese net financial
wealth increased substantially, but average wealth grew
faster. China thus illustrates a central tension of rapid
wealth creation: prosperity can spread while inequality
rises at the same time.
More broadly, the limited movement in both distribution

measures across most countries suggests that wealth
inequality is deeply entrenched. Two decades of
economic growth, crises and political attention have
barely altered the underlying distribution.

China was not alone. The top-decile share increased by

Appendix A: Methodological comments
General assumptions
The Allianz Global Wealth Report analyses gross financial assets held by households, i.e. cash and bank deposits,
receivables from insurance companies and pension institutions, securities (shares, bonds and investment funds) and other
receivables, as well as household liabilities. Net financial assets are defined as gross financial assets minus liabilities.
Real estate is excluded from this measure and analyzed separately where data are available. The report is based

on data from 57 countries. This group of countries covers 91% of global GDP and 72% of the global population. In 43
countries, we had access to statistics from the macroeconomic financial accounts. In the other countries, we were able to
estimate the volume of total financial assets based on information from household surveys, bank statistics, statistics on
assets held in equities and bonds and technical reserves.
To eliminate exchange-rate distortions over time, financial assets and liabilities denominated in national currencies are

converted into euros using exchange rates at the end of 2025. Unless explicitly described as real, these values are not
adjusted for inflation. The closing date for data to be included in the report is 31 July.
Statistical distinctions
The process associated with the introduction of the European System of Accounts 2010 (ESA 2010) in September 2014
involved updating and harmonizing the guidelines governing the preparation of many macroeconomic statistics. The

new requirements also apply to the macroeconomic financial accounts. One change relates to private households: under
the ESVG 2010 regulations, the two sectors „Private households“ and „Non-profit institutions serving households“ are
now reported separately. This also has implications for the Allianz Global Wealth Report, which takes data from the
macroeconomic financial accounts as a basis where available. For many countries, however – particularly those outside

of the EU – there is no separate data available for these sectors in general. So in order to ensure global comparability,
this publication analyzes both sectors together under the heading „private households“.
Determination of wealth bands for global wealth classes
Lower wealth threshold: There is a close link between financial assets and the incomes of private households. According
to Davies et al. (2009) , private individuals with below-average income tend to have no assets at all, or only very few. It is

only when individuals move into middle and higher income groups that they start to accumulate substantial assets.
We have applied this link to our analysis. Countries in the upper-middle income bracket (based on the World Bank‘s
country classification system) therefore form the group in which the average household assets first reach a meaningful
level. This value marks the lower threshold for the global middle wealth class.
In terms of income, households with incomes that correspond to between 75% and 150% of average net income are

generally considered to constitute the middle class. According to Davies et al., households with income corresponding
to 75% of the average income have assets that correspond to 30% of the average assets. As far as the upper threshold
is concerned, 150% of average income corresponds to 180% of average assets. Consequently, we have set the threshold
values for the wealth middle class at 30% and 180% of average net financial assets per capital. In 2025, average net

financial assets per capita in the countries covered stood at approximately EUR36,690. Applying these percentages gives
the rounded thresholds used in the workbook: EUR11,000 and EUR66,000. Individuals with net financial assets between
these thresholds belong to the global middle-wealth class. Those below the lower threshold belong to the low-wealth
class, while those above the upper threshold belong to the high-wealth class.
Appendices
14  Davies, James B. et al. (2009), The level and distribution of global household wealth, NBER working paper 15508.

Document 247: Anthropic拿出5%股权成立公益基金，应对AI冲击就业市场

Source PDF: all report pdf/Anthropic拿出5%股权成立公益基金，应对AI冲击就业市场.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)
Date: 2026-10-19
Pages: 3
Status: ok

### Page 1

Anthropic 还宣布设计动态增持机制 —— 每当估值较基准线上涨 50% ，董事会可追加 2% 股
权。结合创始⼈及员⼯捐赠计划，⽬前约 20% 股份将⽤于公共利益。此举恰逢 Anthropic 计
划 11 ⽉ IPO 、 Q3 营收达 168 亿美元之际，被业内⼈⼠视为兼具必要性与形象价值的战略布
局。
摘要：
AI 领军企业 Anthropic 宣布将公司 5% 股权注⼊⼀只新设公益基⾦，并设计了随公司估值增⻓⽽⾃
动扩⼤持股⽐例的机制，此举将使约 20% 的 Anthropic 股份最终⽤于公共利益。
Anthropic 周⽇（ 10 ⽉ 18 ⽇）宣布，将在股东批准后，向新成⽴的 “Anthropic 公益基⾦ ”
（ Anthropic Public Benefit Fund ）注⼊公司 5% 的股份。 此外，每当公司估值在设定基准线
上⽅每增⻓ 50% ，董事会可酌情追加 2% 的股权。该基⾦将以全资有限责任公司（ LLC ）形式运
营，⽽⾮传统的 501(c)(3) 免税⾮营利机构，以保留在不同捐赠形式之间灵活调配的空间。公司

明确表⽰，该 LLC 持有的股权不得⽤于在美国或其他任何国家的游说或政治捐款。
在⽤途上， Anthropic 将应对 AI 冲击就业市场列为核⼼议题之⼀。公司表⽰， AI 可能创造巨⼤经
济价值，但也可能给⼤量劳动者带来不稳定性。 Anthropic 此前已承诺投⼊ 2 亿美元研究和应对这
⼀问题，但 " 鉴于 AI 进步的速度，可能需要更⼤胆的举措 " 。该基⾦将具备⾜够的灵活性，探索向
传统⾮营利机构进⾏⼤额捐赠，或直接向特定群体发放资⾦等⽅式。
此次公告时机敏感。据 CNBC 此前报道， Anthropic 已向投资者披露第三季度营收达 168 亿美元，
并计划于 11 ⽉启动 IPO 。 IPO 咨询机构 Class V Group 合伙⼈ Lise Buyer 对 CNBC 表⽰，此次公益
基⾦公告在 IPO 准备层⾯具有必要性，同时 “ 从形象⻆度⽽⾔也⼤有裨益 ” ，尤其是在 AI 重塑就业
市场的背景下。
股权结构：越增⻓，公益⽐例越⾼
Anthropic 将此次公益基⾦的股权设计为与公司成⻓挂钩的动态机制。 初始注⼊ 5% 股权，此后

每当公司估值在特定基准线上⽅每提升 50% ，董事会可酌情追加 2% 。
结合此前承诺，这⼀安排将使 Anthropic 的公益股权规模显著扩⼤。公司联合创始⼈在创⽴之初
已承诺捐出各⾃ 80% 的股权持有量；公司还设有员⼯股权捐赠配对计划，约半数员⼯参与其
中。综合上述安排，⽬前约 20% 的 Anthropic 股份将⽤于公共利益，若公司达到特定估值⾥程
碑，这⼀⽐例还将进⼀步提升。
Anthropic 指出，科技公司惯常向⾼管提供递增式股权激励，⽽该公司选择将这部分股权转⽽⽤
于公共利益。
就业冲击： AI 时代的核⼼社会议题
就业市场冲击是 Anthropic 设⽴公益基⾦的重要驱动⼒之⼀。据 CNBC 援引⽪尤研究中⼼（ Pew
Research Center ） 8 ⽉发布的报告， 71% 的美国成年⼈认为 AI 将在未来两年内导致美国就业岗
位减少。
Anthropic 表⽰， AI 可能带来巨⼤经济价值，但同时也可能造成⼤量劳动者的就业不稳定。公司
此前已承诺投⼊2 亿美元专项研究和应对这⼀问题，但认为 “ 鉴于 AI 进步的速度，可能需要更⼤胆

的举措 ” 。

加的估值⾥程碑。公司还将发布⼀份公益报告，就其使命推进情况进⾏公开披露。

Document 257: Belgium’s public finances_ a missed opportunity

Source PDF: all report pdf/Belgium’s public finances_ a missed opportunity.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 2026-10-16
Pages: 3
Status: ok

### Page 1

Belgium’s current failure to agree on a three-year €10bn
savings package is not yet a political crisis, but it is a missed
opportunity. A deal on the 2027 budget remains possible, yet
delaying the harder choices risks weakening the country’s
ﬁscal credibility as the 2029 elections draw closer
Philippe Ledent
Belgian PM Bart De Wever
This was supposed to be a decisive week for Belgium’s public
ﬁnances. The prime minister was due to deliver his general policy

statement to parliament on Tuesday, outlining a budget
agreement among the governing parties for the next three years,
up to the 2029 elections. In addition to measures already adopted,
the government is seeking €10bn in recurring savings, phased in
over the next three years.
A new timetable for negotiations
For now, however, the budget talks have failed to produce an
agreement. A central element of the package was a one-

### Page 2

percentage-point increase in the standard VAT rate, from 21% to
22%, but one coalition party ﬁrmly opposed the proposal. The
general policy statement therefore had to be postponed. Under
Prime Minister Bart De Wever's revised timetable, the immediate
priority is to secure agreement on the 2027 budget, including €4bn
in savings.
Ideally, a deal would be reached by the end of October, allowing
parliament to debate and approve the legislation before year-end.

This would avoid recourse to the system of provisional twelfths,
under which the previous year’s budget is rolled over on a monthly
basis to keep public services running. The PM has made clear that
he wants to avoid such an outcome at all costs. He is even prepared
to push through legislation that would trigger automatic spending
cuts of €3-4bn from January 2027 if no budget is agreed by year-
end.
In a second phase, negotiations are expected to resume with the

aim of ﬁnding a further €6bn in savings across the 2028 and 2029
budgets. This stage will be crucial for Belgium’s medium-term ﬁscal
trajectory. Postponing those discussions until next year is a risk. By
then, the federal and regional elections scheduled for spring 2029
would be closer, and the wide political diﬀerences within the current
coalition would make agreement on additional measures more
diﬃcult.
A chance to stand apart from France
There is no political crisis at this stage. With a package of measures,

Belgium should still be able to adopt a 2027 budget that brings the
deﬁcit below 5% of GDP. Even so, failure to reach a swift and
comprehensive agreement on the full €10bn package is a missed
opportunity for the federal government to show ﬁnancial markets
that Belgium is not France.
On headline indicators – public deﬁcits, debt and even economic
growth – little separates the two countries. France’s diﬃculty,
however, lies in its inability to set and pass a budget, compounded

by the political uncertainty surrounding the April 2027 presidential
election. Belgium, by contrast, has a governing majority capable of

Document 269: Capital Market Outlook (2026-10-05)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Capital Market Outlook (2026-10-05).pdf
Report source: Merrill Lynch (CMO)
Date: 2026-10-05
Pages: 8
Status: ok

### Page 1

CHIEF INVESTMENT OFFICE
Capital Market Outlook

### Page 2

2 of 8 October 5, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MACRO STRATEGY
Stronger Growth, Costlier Money
Chief Investment Office, Macro Strategy Team
The latest GDP report and annual revisions show a sturdier U.S. economy than previously
reported. According to the Bureau of Economic Analysis (BEA), real GDP grew at a 2.2%
annualized pace in Q2 compared to 1.5% previously estimated, and Q1 was revised
from 2.1% to 2.5%. Both 2024 and 2025 were also revised slightly higher. Most of the

first half of the year revision was in categories related to the Artificial Intelligence (AI)
buildout, but real consumer spending has also been revised slightly higher, accelerating
from 0.7% annualized growth in Q1 to 3.8% in Q2 despite surging energy prices and
slow hiring.
Spending in July and August remained much stronger than labor market conditions alone
would suggest, also averaging 3.7% annualized growth. Upside revisions to net interest

income and personal saving suggest households have benefited from more income than
was previously apparent, with personal saving revised higher back to 2023. The new data,
according to the BEA, show the January to July 2026 savings rate averaged 4.8% versus
3.3% prerevision, strengthening consumer spending underpinnings despite moderate
hiring, high energy prices and rising borrowing costs.
The BEA update also included inflation methodological changes related to categories

inflated by the AI boom—such as portfolio management fees and computer software and
accessories. These improved the inflation picture to some extent—August “core” PCE
inflation was reduced by 0.3 ppts to 3% YoY, bringing it closer to “core” consumer price
index inflation, which was 2.5% YoY and 2% on a three-month annualized basis. While still
above the 2% Federal Reserve (Fed) target, “core” PCE now appears stable at 3% year over
year since January rather than accelerating. These methodological changes are expected

to dampen pass-through of AI-related price pressures into “core” inflation and effects of
stock-market swings on inflation.
They also produced an encouraging improvement in recent PCE inflation momentum.
Three-month annualized “core” PCE inflation slowed from 4.3% at the peak of tariff
effects in February to 2.1% in August. For durable goods, this rate was down from 5.8%
in February to 1.5% in August, and, for services ex-energy and healthcare, it slowed from

4.2% to 2.2%, for example. Inflation is volatile on a three-month change basis, so it
remains to be seen whether this moderation continues without further Fed tightening.
For now, the trends in the San Francisco Fed’s cyclical versus acyclical inflation measures
diverged again in August. As expected, given softening wage growth, prices most sensitive
to the labor market have eased further. At 3% YoY, this inflation category is down from
8% in early 2023 and 4.6% two years ago is back to prepandemic levels and is likely

to ease more (Exhibit 1A). In contrast, supply bottlenecks, high energy prices and other
industry-specific factors have kept acyclical inflation on an uptrend. As shown in Exhibit
1B, the relentless increase in freight costs could prolong upside pressure on this category.
The diesel market has tightened in recent months, boosting its price by 70% YoY,
consistent with Brent oil at around $200 per barrel and large reverberations across
a wide swath of goods and services prices. A government-policy-related decline in

available truck drivers is also raising transportation costs more than expected. So are
tight ocean shipping capacity and longer/more expensive voyages due to the U.S./Iran
War. Supply-chain stress also increased as companies rushed to front-run potential new
tariff increases.
On the positive side for the inflation outlook, unit labor costs (ULC) are up modestly
as robust productivity growth has absorbed much of the wage growth, limiting a major
source of domestic inflation. Upside productivity growth revisions are likely to further

reduce ULC, which could help shift focus from energy and AI-related inflation to
eventual disinflationary effects once oil and refined fuel prices start to ease. Sustained
productivity gains would support growth and margins while limiting inflation and the
need for much higher interest rates.
Investment Implications
We favor selective Equity exposure
to companies with durable demand
and pricing power, while remaining
cautious about businesses most

exposed to fuel, freight, and
financing costs. We would also
gradually increase long-duration
exposure.

### Page 3

3 of 8 October 5, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
Surging nominal growth has boosted corporate revenues (up 13% YoY in Q2, according to the
BEA) while contained unit labor costs helped raise margins and profits more than expected.
With labor costs declining as a share of revenues, investment-related tax incentives and
rock-bottom net interest expense, pre-tax nonfinancial corporate profits as measured in GDP
accounts rose 21% YoY in Q2. Total corporate profits after-tax were up 18.2% YoY, according to

the BEA.
Surging AI-related imports and investment are keeping the business and profits cycle on
an uptrend both here and abroad. According to BofA Global Research, the global economic
cycle backdrop improved further in September, as reflected in a broad-based increase in its
Global Wave1 indicator. Broadening U.S. manufacturing strength, fueled by ongoing AI-driven
investment, a healthier orders backlog and low inventories suggest that the expansion has

room to run—a constructive environment for earnings. Typically, a Global Wave upswing is
consistent with rising earnings revisions ratios and equity price uptrends. According to the
Global Wave, global earnings growth expectations remain exceptionally strong.
Still, absent a resolution of war-related energy and transportation disruptions, the same
industrial strength intensifies inflation pressures. A prolonged supply shock raises the risk of
restrictive interest rates and weakening economic growth. In this context, the surge in the

10-year Treasury yield above 5% is starting to reflect more than optimism about U.S. growth
and strengthening productivity outlook. Oil’s tightest recent correlation with 10-year Treasury
yields in 35 years suggests a common energy-price thread behind rising yields in developed
countries.
Bond market spillovers from normalizing Japan yields and capital repatriation are another
common thread. Higher yields on foreign sovereign bonds such as in Japan—which has

escaped stagnation and deflation by intensely pursuing investment-led growth—weaken the
relative appeal of Treasurys. That distinction matters for portfolios. A yield increase driven
mainly by durable productivity gains would come with a stronger outlook for future income
and earnings. A yield increase driven by cost shocks and greater uncertainty raises financing
costs without the same benefit.
In sum, the economy remains more resilient than expected, but the bond market is also

charging more for an uncertain inflation outlook given untethered government debt and
cooling global demand for Treasurys spurred by geopolitical tensions and Japan capital
repatriation. The case for much further tightening becomes stronger if wage growth
strengthens, productivity lags, and unit labor costs reaccelerate, causing cyclical inflation to
also pick up steam. The case becomes weaker if that measure stays contained while fuel and
freight pressures recede or rising Treasury yields reduce asset prices and economic growth.

MARKET VIEW
Yields, Yen and Iran: What Haunts the “House”
Lauren Sanfilippo, Director and Senior Investment Strategist
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
We recently highlighted the three-front challenge facing Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent:
engineering a stronger yen, containing the 10-year Treasury yield and securing a diplomatic
resolution to the conflict with Iran.

Bessent recently has delivered one of the year’s more memorable lines while discussing
Treasury policy and the yen: “I have asymmetric information. I am the house now.” 2 Yet several
challenges remain: the 10-year Treasury yield is near levels last seen in the early 2000s, the
yen remains structurally weaker, and a phased agreement to reopen the Strait of Hormuz
appears remote. The resulting strain on energy markets keeps inflation risks elevated.

Put differently, oil prices, Treasury yields and the dollar-yen exchange rate are all trending
against Bessent’s objectives. Below, we examine how each variable is haunting his “house.”
Haunting the “House” is the Yen. Since the U.S. and Japan mounted a record $98.7B
coordinated intervention in late July after the yen weakened to a 40-year low near 164 per
dollar, the currency has appreciated roughly 4% against the dollar. The move was further
supported on September 18 when the BoJ raised rates by 25 basis points (bps) to 1.25%,

their highest level since the mid-1990s (Exhibit 2A). However, the split 7–2 vote and Governor
Ueda’s limited forward guidance leave the path of further tightening uncertain. The key
question is whether additional BoJ hikes can produce sustained appreciation in the yen, or
merely reinforce the temporary relief provided by the summer intervention.
Bessent, meanwhile, has repeatedly signaled his preference for a stronger yen. Partly because
yen stability is also a U.S. financing concern. Persistent currency weakness could prompt

Japan to support its currency by selling foreign assets, including U.S. Treasurys—which would
be an unwelcome prospect as Washington partially relies on foreign capital flows to fund its
large fiscal deficits. That potential “haunts” the Treasury Secretary.  At precisely the moment
Washington needs foreign capital to finance its enormous fiscal deficits, it would be inopportune
for one of America’s largest creditors to become a significant seller of Treasury securities.

Haunting the “House” are Yields. The surge in yields runs counter to a White House intent
on lowering borrowing costs. The benchmark 10-year yield has broken above the 5% threshold
and climbed to levels not seen since 2007. At around 5.30%, it stands more than a full
percentage point above where it began the year (Exhibit 2B). The level is striking but so is the
speed of the move: Stronger-than-expected economic data, including a survey showing U.S.

2 September 8, 2026.

complicating the macro backdrop
(i.e., higher-for-longer yields/oil)
the combination of strong earnings

that the fundamental backdrop for

5 of 8 October 5, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
business activity expanded at the fastest pace in more than five years, helped trigger an 18
bps surge in the 10-year yield—among its largest daily moves in decades. The 5-year yield also
topped 5% for the first time since 2007, while the 30-year yield reached 5.63%, its highest
level since 2002.
Compounding the move is inflation, running above the Fed’s 2% target for 67 months,

large fiscal deficits and the prospect of continued growth in Treasury issuance. Against this
backdrop, Treasury bought back $4.1 billion of long-dated debt, versus a maximum of $6
billion. That effort has done little to arrest the selloff. A subsequent auction priced nearly $4
billion of 7-year notes at 5.085%, the highest yield for that maturity in 33 years.
All in, the MOVE index, a key gauge of Treasury market volatility, jumped from 78 to 95 on

Wednesday September 23 and then to 104 the following day, its highest level since the early
days of the Iran war (Exhibit 3A).
Haunting the “House” is the Iran conflict. Early expectations that military pressure and
economic sanctions would quickly bring Iran to the negotiating table have yet to materialize.
Iranian officials have signaled that a full reopening of the Strait of Hormuz remains unlikely
while sanctions stay in place. Although talks between Washington and Tehran are continuing,

conflicting signals and lingering skepticism from both sides have kept tensions elevated.
As a result, oil prices have remained elevated in a range even as some Middle East supply
disruptions began to ease.
Startling is that Brent crude is up more than 60% this year, but refined product prices
have risen even faster. Ongoing disruptions to energy flows through the Strait of Hormuz,
combined with Ukrainian strikes on Russian refining infrastructure have tightened global

supplies of crude oil and refined fuels. Saudi Arabia has restored some drone-disrupted flows,
providing roughly 3.5 million barrels a day of relief (roughly 3% of global consumption).
The squeeze is mostly acute in diesel markets, where inventories remain constrained and
prices continue to climb (Exhibit 3B). U.S. diesel prices recently surpassed $6.50 per gallon,
the highest level on record, just as Russia has extended restrictions on diesel exports into

October. Against this backdrop, proposals to ban U.S. fuel exports risks worsening the
imbalance. According to S&P Global, an export ban could reduce refinery runs by nearly 2
million barrels per day and significantly cut gasoline production, potentially driving gasoline
and jet fuel prices even higher. As long as tensions persist, the oil impulse remains a key
impediment to structurally lower inflation.
Investment implications. Treasury Secretary Bessent increasingly sits at the intersection of

monetary policy, fiscal policy, foreign policy and national security. We remain constructive on
the U.S. economy and overweight U.S. Equities but are watching as these fronts challenge the
narrative. There is a great deal riding on the “house” versus the key macro variables of today.

6 of 8 October 5, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
THOUGHT OF THE WEEK
When It Comes to Attracting Foreign Capital, the U.S. Remains Exceptional
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
There may be no better vote of confidence in a country than the willingness of
foreigners to put their money there. And by that measurement, the U.S. remains the
exception—both portfolio and FDI inflows are currently running at record highs based

on the latest data. This dynamic is one key reason why we remain constructive on U.S.
assets over the longer term.
Notwithstanding all the handwringing about America’s fiscal deficits, tariffs and rising
government debt, global investors sunk some $942B in net purchases of U.S. Equities in
the 12 months to July, the highest rolling 12-month total on record. In Q2, net purchase
of U.S. Equities soared to $426B, up 62% from the same period a year ago (Exhibit 4A).

By country, flows were down from China—not surprisingly—but strong from Taiwan and
South Korea, who have taken to recycling their massive AI-generated trade surplus back
into the U.S. In the end, strong portfolio inflows suggest that international investors
remain willing to increase their exposure to the world's largest capital market—that’s
bullish for U.S. assets.
Meanwhile, Exhibit 4B highlights the growing push among foreign multinationals to be

inside the U.S., with FDI inflows totaling nearly $240B in the first six months of this year.
That is a staggering 103% jump from a year ago and means FDI inflows are on track for
a record year, estimated at $480B for 2026.
There are multiple factors behind surging inflows: One, due to rising U.S. trade
protectionism, foreign multinationals increasingly want to be “inside” the U.S. economy,
not outside. Two, within the $32 trillion-plus economy with extraordinarily consumer

purchasing power, foreign firms prefer to be closer to their customers via investment
than just trade (exports). Three, America’s massive investment cycle in AI, data centers,
semiconductors, power generation, etc., has created numerous potential investment
opportunities for many foreign firms that don’t want to miss out on America’s surging
capex cycle. Four, and finally, abundant energy supplies, deep capital markets and a highly
developed technological ecosystem have also induced more FDI inflows.

In the end, double-barreled capital inflows speak to the absolute and relative
attractiveness of the U.S. economy and capital markets. Stay invested in America—the
rest of the world is.

Index Definitions
Securities indexes assume reinvestment of all distributions and interest payments. Indexes are unmanaged and do not take into account fees or expenses. It is not possible to
invest directly in an index. Indexes are all based in U.S. dollars.
S&P 500 Index is a stock market index tracking the stock performance of 500 leading companies listed on stock exchanges in the United States.

ICE BofA MOVE Index is a market-implied measure of U.S. bond market volatility.
Consumer Price Index measures the average change over time in the prices paid by urban consumers for a market basket of consumer goods and services.
Baltic Dry Index is a daily shipping and trade index published by the Baltic Exchange in London that measures the cost of moving raw materials like iron ore, coal, and grain by sea.
Volatility Index (VIX) is a real-time market indicator that measures the stock market's expected volatility over the next 30 days.

Bank of America, Merrill, their affiliates and advisors do not provide legal, tax or accounting advice. Clients should consult their legal and/or tax advisors before making any financial decisions.
This material does not take into account a client’s particular investment objectives, financial situations, or needs and is not intended as a recommendation, offer, or solicitation for the purchase
or sale of any security or investment strategy. Merrill offers a broad range of brokerage, investment advisory and other services. There are important differences between brokerage and

of America, Merrill or any affiliate to sell or provide, or a specific invitation for a consumer to apply for, any particular retail financial product or service that may be available.
The Chief Investment Office (“CIO”) provides thought leadership on wealth management, investment strategy and global markets; portfolio management solutions; due diligence; and solutions
oversight and data analytics. CIO viewpoints are developed for Bank of America Private Bank, a division of Bank of America, N.A., (“Bank of America”) and Merrill Lynch, Pierce, Fenner & Smith

The Global Wealth & Investment Management Investment Strategy Committee (“GWIM ISC”) is responsible for developing and coordinating recommendations for short-term and long-term
investment strategy and market views encompassing markets, economic indicators, asset classes and other market-related projections affecting GWIM.
BofA Global Research is research produced by BofA Securities, Inc. (“BofAS”) and/or one or more of its affiliates. BofAS is a registered broker-dealer, Member SIPC and wholly owned subsidiary

of Bank of America Corporation.
All recommendations must be considered in the context of an individual investor’s goals, time horizon, liquidity needs and risk tolerance. Not all recommendations will be in the best interest
of all investors.
Asset allocation, diversification and rebalancing do not ensure a profit or protect against loss in declining markets.
Investments have varying degrees of risk. Some of the risks involved with equity securities include the possibility that the value of the stocks may fluctuate in response to events specific to

the companies or markets, as well as economic, political or social events in the U.S. or abroad. Small cap and mid cap companies pose special risks, including possible illiquidity and greater price
volatility than funds consisting of larger, more established companies. Investing in fixed-income securities may involve certain risks, including the credit quality of individual issuers, possible
prepayments, market or economic developments and yields and share price fluctuations due to changes in interest rates. When interest rates go up, bond prices typically drop, and vice versa.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.
Alternative investments are speculative and involve a high degree of risk.
Alternative investments are intended for qualified investors only. Alternative Investments such as derivatives, hedge funds, private credit, private equity funds, and funds of funds can result

in higher return potential but also higher loss potential. Changes in economic conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect your investments. Before you invest in alternative
investments, you should consider your overall financial situation, how much money you have to invest, your need for liquidity, and your tolerance for risk.

### Page 1

China's third-quarter GDP growth of 4.5% will likely keep this
year's growth target on track. September data showed signs of
stabilisation, though domestic demand remains
underwhelming

4.5% YoY China's 3Q GDP
Higher than expected
China's 3Q GDP comes in slightly stronger
than expectations
China's GDP rebounded to 4.5% year-on-year in the third quarter,
up from 4.3% in the second quarter, a little stronger than expected

### Page 2

(market: 4.4%, ING: 4.4%). This brings the year-to-date GDP growth
down to 4.6% YoY.
By industry, China's tertiary sector continued to grow fastest for
the sixth consecutive quarter, up 5.2% YoY in 3Q, versus 3.6% and
3.4% for the primary and secondary industries, respectively. As
China's economy matures, the services sector will play an even
larger role.
A strong third-quarter trade surplus drove China's rebound. Final
consumption expenditure's contribution to GDP, at 2.148pp, was at

its lowest since 4Q22.
With a few months left in the year, we believe China's economy is
well positioned to achieve its 2026 growth target of 4.5-5.0%.
However, the economy remains quite unbalanced heading into
2027, leaving growth vulnerable to disruptions in external demand.
Fortunately, we also saw some favourable signs from the
September domestic activity data.
GDP growth should end the year on target
September's domestic data showed signs of

stabilisation
September's domestic activity data generally improved from
August, contributing to the 3Q26 GDP outperformance.
Below are our takeaways from the major data releases:

### Page 3

Retail sales growth was stronger than forecasts (market: 0.5%,
ING: 0.6%), rising to 1.1% YoY from 0.4%. Strong
communication appliance (31.1%) sales drove the data, likely
beneﬁting from a host of new phone model launches. Beyond
this category, sales of consumer staples such as tobacco and
alcohol (13.8%), grains & oils (6.7%), and beverages (5.0%) all
outperformed. However, many discretionary consumption
categories, such as gold and jewellery (-9.6%) and autos

(-21.2%), remained weak.
Fixed asset investment growth stabilised at -7.2% YoY after
decelerating every month since March, coming in stronger than
expectations (market: -7.3%, ING: -7.5%). This stabilisation
reﬂects public-led (-3.2%) and manufacturing (-2.1%) FAI
picking up on the month, oﬀsetting slowdowns in private-led
(-10.4%), real estate (-20.6%), and infrastructure (-4.2%) FAI.
Investment has clearly been a major drag this year, so
stabilisation is encouraging, possibly as eﬀorts to accelerate

ﬁscal spending outlined at July's Politburo meeting begin to
take eﬀect.
Industrial production continues to be the main outperformer,
up 5.5% YoY from 5.2% in August, in line with market
expectations (market: 5.5%, ING: 5.5%). Hi-tech manufacturing
(15.3%) remains a bright spot, and export-oriented industries
such as computers and communications (16.4%), rail, ships,
and aerospace (11.7%), and autos (6.7%) continue to
outperform.
70-city property prices continued to drop, but by less than

what we saw in August's data. New home prices (-0.14% MoM)
fell the least in 17 months. Prices stabilised or rose in 21 cities.
Existing home prices (-0.26% MoM) saw the smallest drop in 4
months, with prices stabilising or rising in 11 cities. Tier 1 cities
continued to perform well on the month, with a few tier 2 cities
joining the price recovery.
Compared with rapid export growth, domestic activity continues to
lag. But September's data at least shows modest positive

momentum heading into 4Q26.

Document 283: CTCP Dịch vụ Hàng Không Taseco - Báo cáo cập nhật 2Q2026 (2026-09-22)

Source PDF: all report pdf/CTCP Dịch vụ Hàng Không Taseco - Báo cáo cập nhật 2Q2026 (2026-09-22).pdf
Report source: KBSV (Company)
Date: 2026-09-22
Pages: 10
Status: ok

### Page 1

CTCP Dịch vụ Hàng không Taseco (AST) 
Tiếp tục mở rộng điểm kinh doanh 
22/09/2026  
Chuyên viên cao cấp Nguyễn Ngọc Anh 
(+84) 24-7303-5333 anhntn@kbsec.com.vn

KQKD tăng trưởng tích cực trong 
2Q2026

Tăng trưởng khách qua cảng hàng 
không thúc đẩy doanh thu tại các 
điểm kinh doanh hiện hữu

Mở rộng mạng lưới tạo động lực tăng 
trưởng doanh thu mới

Cơ cấu kinh doanh và hiệu quả vận 
hành hỗ trợ cải thiện biên lợi nhuận

Chúng tôi khuyến nghị MUA, giá mục 
tiêu 76,500 VND/cổ phiếu

2Q2026, AST ghi nhận doanh thu và LNST đạt 479/92 tỷ VND (+22%/19% yoy), với 
động lực chủ yếu đến từ (1) đóng góp từ 19 điểm kinh doanh mở mới tại T3 Tân Sơn 
Nhất và (2) tăng trưởng tích cực của các mảng hàng miễn thuế, nhà hàng - fastfood 
và phòng chờ thương gia. Lũy kế 6T2026, doanh thu và LNST đạt 1,035/221 tỷ VND 
(+33%/46% yoy), lần lượt hoàn thành 48%/49% kế hoạch năm.

Chúng tôi dự phóng lượng khách quốc tế/nội địa tại các thị trường AST khai thác 
tăng bình quân 11%/5% mỗi năm đến 2030. Lưu lượng và tỷ trọng khách quốc tế 
gia tăng được kỳ vọng thúc đẩy số giao dịch và doanh thu bình quân trên mỗi 
khách, đặc biệt tại mảng miễn thuế, bán lẻ và phòng chờ.

Chúng tôi dự phóng số điểm kinh doanh AST tăng từ 145 điểm cuối năm 2026 lên 
182 điểm năm 2030. Sau đóng góp từ 19 điểm tại T3 Tân Sơn Nhất và 6 điểm mới tại 
T2 Nội Bài từ 2025 - 2026, các dự án Long Thành, Phú Quốc và Gia Bình tiếp tục mở 
ra dư địa mở rộng mạng lưới trong trung và dài hạn cho doanh nghiệp.

Việc mở rộng các hoạt động mang lại lợi nhuận cao, bao gồm hàng miễn thuế, 
phòng chờ thương gia và tối ưu hoạt động tại các điểm kinh doanh được kỳ vọng 
tác động tích cực lên biên lợi nhuận của AST. Chúng tôi dự phóng biên lợi nhuận 
gộp đạt 64.7%/65.5% trong năm 2026/2027, hỗ trợ LNST công ty mẹ tăng lần lượt 
48%/17% yoy.

Chúng tôi khuyến nghị MUA đối với AST, giá mục tiêu 76,500 VND/cổ phiếu, tương 
đương tiềm năng tăng giá 20% so với giá đóng cửa ngày 21/09/2026.

Cơ cấu doanh thu 2025 Hoạt động kinh doanh

CTCP Dịch vụ Hàng không Taseco là một 
trong những doanh nghiệp có quy mô mạng 
lưới điểm kinh doanh lớn trong lĩnh vực dịch 
vụ phi hàng không tại Việt Nam, với 141 
điểm kinh doanh tại 6 sân bay quốc tế tính 
đến cuối tháng 6/2026. Hoạt động cốt lõi của 
công ty bao gồm bán lẻ bách hóa lưu niệm 
(BHLN), hàng miễn thuế, dịch vụ ăn uống và 
phòng chờ thương gia. Ngoài ra, AST còn 
tham gia lĩnh vực suất ăn hàng không thông 
qua khoản đầu tư tại công ty liên kết VinaCS. 
Nguồn: Báo cáo công ty, KBSV

Điểm nhấn đầu tư  Chú thích 
Doanh thu trên các điểm kinh doanh hiện hữu tăng nhờ lượng khách quốc tế/nội địa dự phóng tăng 
11%/5% mỗi năm đến 2030. 
 Chi tiết ở phần dưới của báo cáo 
Mở rộng mạng lưới tạo động lực tăng trưởng mới, với số điểm kinh doanh AST 2026 dự kiến đạt 145 
điểm và tăng lên 182 điểm vào 2030. 
 Chi tiết ở phần dưới của báo cáo 
Biên lợi nhuận gộp cải thiện lên 64.7%/65.5% trong năm 2026/2027 nhờ mở rộng hàng miễn thuế, 
phòng chờ và tối ưu vận hành. 
 Chi tiết ở phần dưới của báo cáo

HOẠT ĐỘNG KINH DOANH 
KQKD tăng trưởng tích cực trong 
2Q2026

2Q2026, AST ghi nhận doanh thu và LNST đạt 479/92 tỷ VND (+22%/19% yoy), 
với động lực: (1) đóng góp từ 19 điểm kinh doanh mở mới tại T3 Tân Sơn Nhất 
và (2) tăng trưởng tích cực của các mảng hàng miễn thuế, nhà hàng - fastfood 
và phòng chờ thương gia. Lũy kế 6T2026, doanh thu và LNST đạt 1,035/221 tỷ 
VND (+33%/46% yoy), lần lượt hoàn thành 48%/49% kế hoạch năm.

Biểu đồ 5. Doanh thu/điểm kinh doanh của AST

Nguồn:AST

Tăng trưởng khách qua cảng hàng 
không thúc đẩy doanh thu tại các 
điểm kinh doanh hiện hữu 
 Chúng tôi kỳ vọng tăng trưởng lưu lượng hành khách, đặc biệt là sự gia tăng tỷ 
trọng khách quốc tế, sẽ thúc đẩy doanh thu và hiệu quả kinh doanh của AST 
trong giai đoạn 2026–2030. Theo Cục Hàng không Việt Nam, lượng khách thông 
qua các cảng hàng không trong 7T2026 đạt hơn 76 triệu lượt (+7% yoy), trong 
đó khách quốc tế đạt gần 30 triệu lượt (+11% yoy), trong khi khách nội địa ghi 
nhận tăng trưởng 4% yoy. Xu hướng này được hỗ trợ bởi nhu cầu du lịch quốc 
tế, chính sách thị thực thuận lợi và việc mở rộng mạng bay, trong khi tăng 
trưởng kinh tế và thu nhập tiếp tục tạo nền tảng cho nhu cầu đi lại trong nước. 
Trên cơ sở đó, chúng tôi dự phóng lượt khách quốc tế và nội địa tại các thị 
trường AST khai thác tăng bình quân lần lượt 11% và 5%/năm đến 2030, qua đó 
nâng dần tỷ trọng khách quốc tế trong tổng lưu lượng, thúc đẩy doanh 
thu/điểm kinh doanh nhờ: (1) lưu lượng hành khách qua cảng hàng không gia 
tăng và (2) tỷ trọng khách quốc tế cao hơn hỗ trợ doanh thu bình quân trên mỗi 
khách, đặc biệt tại mảng miễn thuế, bán lẻ và phòng chờ.

### Page 5

Biểu đồ 6. Lượt khách qua cảng hàng không Việt Nam (triệu 
lượt) 
 Biểu đồ 7. Doanh thu/điểm kinh doanh AST (tỷ VND)

Nguồn: AST  Nguồn: AST

Mở rộng mạng lưới tại các nhà ga 
hiện hữu và sân bay mới, gia tăng 
quy mô kinh doanh đến 2030 
 Chúng tôi kỳ vọng chu kỳ mở rộng hạ tầng hàng không giai đoạn 2026–2030 sẽ 
tạo dư địa gia tăng quy mô kinh doanh cho AST, với số điểm kinh doanh dự 
phóng đạt 145 điểm vào cuối 2026 và tăng lên 182 điểm vào năm 2030. Cụ thể: 
- Trong ngắn hạn, tăng trưởng năm 2026 được hỗ trợ bởi việc ghi nhận 
đủ năm doanh thu từ 19 điểm kinh doanh mở mới tại T3 Tân Sơn Nhất 
trong năm 2025, cùng 6 điểm mới tại T2 Nội Bài trong năm 2026, trong 
đó có 2 cửa hàng miễn thuế và 2 cửa hàng Memories of Vietnam khai 
trương trong 2Q2026. 
- Trong trung và dài hạn, các dự án Cảng HKQT Long Thành, Cảng HKQT 
Phú Quốc nâng cấp mở rộng và Cảng HKQT Gia Bình sẽ góp phần thúc 
đẩy ngành hàng không Việt Nam nói chung, bổ sung mặt bằng thương 
mại, mở ra cơ hội phát triển các mảng kinh doanh cốt lõi cho AST nói 
riêng bao gồm các mảng bán lẻ, miễn thuế, nhà hàng - fastfood và 
phòng chờ thương gia. Với kinh nghiệm khai thác 141 điểm kinh doanh 
tại 6 sân bay quốc tế và lịch sử trúng thầu tại T3 Tân Sơn Nhất, T2 Nội 
Bài trong thời gian qua, chúng tôi kỳ vọng AST có khả năng cạnh tranh 
cao trong việc đấu thầu quyền khai thác mặt bằng tại các sân bay mới, 
đặc biệt là Gia Bình, qua đó duy trì vị thế và thị phần kinh doanh phi 
hàng không tại khu vực phía Bắc khi một phần lưu lượng hành khách 
được phân bổ lại từ Nội Bài.

Cơ cấu kinh doanh và hiệu quả vận 
hành hỗ trợ mở rộng biên lợi nhuận  
 Chúng tôi kỳ vọng biên lợi nhuận của AST tiếp tục cải thiện trong 2026 - 2027, 
nhờ định hướng mở rộng các mảng có biên lợi nhuận tốt như hàng miễn thuế 
và phòng chờ thương gia, kết hợp tối ưu hiệu quả vận hành tại các điểm kinh 
doanh. Cụ thể: (1) AST ưu tiên gia tăng số điểm kinh doanh hàng miễn thuế 
nhằm đón đầu tăng trưởng nhanh và ổn định của lượng khách quốc tế, qua đó 
nâng đóng góp lợi nhuận từ hoạt động mua sắm tại sân bay, (2) mở rộng mạng 
lưới phòng chờ, đồng thời gia tăng lượng khách sử dụng trên nền năng lực phục 
vụ hiện hữu và (3) tối ưu hóa hiệu quả hoạt động thông qua cải thiện bố trí mặt 
bằng, danh mục sản phẩm và kiểm soát chi phí tại các điểm kinh doanh, qua 
đó tăng doanh thu và lợi nhuận thu được trên mỗi khách hàng.

Chúng tôi khuyến nghị MUA, giá 
mục tiêu 76,500 VND/cổ phiếu 
 Chúng tôi khuyến nghị MUA đối với cổ phiếu AST, với giá mục tiêu 76,500 
VND/cổ phiếu, tương đương tiềm năng tăng giá 20% so với giá đóng cửa ngày 
21/09/2026, dựa trên triển vọng tăng trưởng từ lượng khách quốc tế, mở rộng 
mạng lưới điểm kinh doanh và khả năng cải thiện biên lợi nhuận của doanh 
nghiệp. Chúng tôi sử dụng phương pháp định giá từng phần (SOTP), bao gồm 
DCF cho hoạt động kinh doanh cốt lõi của AST và P/E mục tiêu 11x đối với phần 
lợi nhuận thuộc sở hữu AST tại VinaCS. Các giả định chính gồm: (1) lượng khách 
quốc tế/nội địa tăng trung bình 11%/5% mỗi năm đến 2030, (2) số điểm kinh 
doanh tăng từ 145 điểm cuối 2026 lên 182 điểm năm 2030, với giả định AST 
trúng thầu mặt bằng tại cả Long Thành và Gia Bình và (3) biên lợi nhuận gộp 
2026/2027 đạt 64.7/65.5%. Đối với VinaCS, chúng tôi kỳ vọng nhà máy suất ăn 
Long Thành sẽ hỗ trợ tăng trưởng lợi nhuận trong trung và dài hạn khi dự kiến 
có thể bắt đầu đóng góp lợi nhuận cho AST từ 2028.

Rủi ro đối với định giá bao gồm: (1) AST không giành được quyền khai thác tại 
Long Thành và Gia Bình như kỳ vọng, (2) lưu lượng khách dịch chuyển giữa các 
sân bay mới và hiện hữu khiến doanh thu tại một số điểm kinh doanh bị sụt 
giảm, (3) cạnh tranh gia tăng từ các doanh nghiệp kinh doanh dịch vụ phi hàng 
không khác và (4) căng thẳng địa chính trị kéo dài, giá dầu tăng gây áp lực lên 
hoạt động hàng không và chi tiêu của hành khách.

Document 305: First Impressions_ NZIER Quarterly Survey of Business Opinion, September quarter 2026 (2026-10-20)

Source PDF: all report pdf/First Impressions_ NZIER Quarterly Survey of Business Opinion, September quarter 2026 (2026-10-20).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-10-20
Pages: 10
Status: ok

### Page 1

#businessconditions #newzealand
First Impressions: NZIER Quarterly Survey of Business Opinion,
September quarter 2026
By Michael Gordon
Senior Economist, Westpac NZ
08:35 October 06 2026 Share
Businesses are feeling more optimistic about the months ahead, but current conditions
remain tough for many industries. Cost pressures eased off a little compared to last quarter.
Key results (seasonally adjusted)
General business situation: +40 (Prev: +14)

### Page 2

Trading activity, past three months: -1 (Prev: +1) 
Trading activity, next three months: +15 (Prev: +11) 
Average selling prices, past three months: +27 (Prev: +37) 
Average selling prices, next three months: +45 (Prev: +51)

The NZIER’s quarterly survey of business opinion showed a strong lift in general confidence in
September, despite resurgent fuel prices and the uncertainty around the looming election. Firms were
more confident about their prospects for the next few months, even though their recent performance
remained rough in many industries. 
The survey was held over a relatively compressed period (9 to 28 September), with around 40% of
responses coming back on the first day. Later responses were less confident, as the resurgence in
world oil prices was finally reflected in prices at the pump over the course of the month. 
General business sentiment rose to a net 40% positive compared to a net 14% in June, returning to
around the levels seen in the December quarter last year (before the Middle East conflict). However,
the own-activity measures, which tend to have a closer correspondence with GDP growth, were more
subdued. Trading activity over the last three months was slightly weaker than in June, while
expectations for the quarter ahead saw a more modest four-point increase to +15%. 
Other forward-looking measures were also up for the quarter. Hiring intentions rose strongly to a net
23% positive (although past employment was down). Notably, firms continue to report that workers are
becoming harder to find, despite the ongoing rise in unemployment over the last couple of years. That
said, the proportion of firms citing labour as a factor limiting output declined this quarter. Investment
intentions picked up, more so for plant and machinery than for buildings. 
The results were mixed across industries. Manufacturing and services were more upbeat for the
months ahead but reported substantially weaker performance over the last quarter. In contrast, builders
and merchants reported both stronger past results and greater optimism about the near-term outlook. 
The pricing measures in the survey were down compared to June – not entirely surprising as fuel
prices were lower on average through the September quarter compared to the June quarter. The
notable exception was merchants, who expected both higher costs and prices for the quarter ahead. 
The implications for the RBNZ ahead of its 28 October policy review are unclear – there is certainly no
smoking gun in this report that would demand that the RBNZ abandon the “pause” strategy
communicated at the September MPS. Optimism among businesses remains high despite the obvious
headwinds, but there is still a wide gap between hopes and reality. The pricing measures have eased
compared to last quarter, but that may simply confirm the RBNZ’s expectation that annual inflation will
drop from the 4.1% recorded last quarter as fuel prices eased. The September quarter CPI, released
on 22 October, may be more informative in that respect. 
Michael Gordon, Senior Economist
P: +64 21 749 506

崇越 5434 TT  ( 電子–電子通路 ) 
先進製程擴張為營運成長基礎 
投資與評價 
1. 光阻劑、矽晶圓、光罩等半導體核心材料需求持續強勁，另環保工程業
務受惠於大型專案認列，2Q26 營收優於我們先前預期。 
2. 綜觀 7~9 月營收皆維持高檔，研究部推估主要受惠於晶圓代工業產能
持續擴張，且另有漲價挹注營收表現，3Q26 營收為 236.7 億元。 
3. 半導體主力產品及新品需求皆見成長，帶動半導體材料業務持續維持季
增，研究部上調 FY26 光阻劑營收全年年增率達 30%以上。 
4. 研究部上調 FY26/FY27 營收獲利預估，預估 FY26/FY27 EPS 分別為 
30.72/32.34 元。研究部維持買進投資建議，目標價調升至 700 元，以
21x FY27 獲利預估。 
重點更新 
半導體材料業務仍為今明年成長主旋律 
半導體主力產品及新品需求皆見成長，帶動半導體材料業務持續維持季增。
研究部上調 FY26 光阻劑營收全年年增率達 30%以上。矽晶圓市場需求情況
逐漸轉好 。 藍寶石基板 、 散熱材料等新品亦開始於1H26 成長力道逐漸顯著 ，
使其他電子材料 1H26 年增率達 30%以上。研究部預期產品調漲效應將延續
至 4Q26， 帶動公司FY26 整體營運逐季走高 ，FY27 營運表現亦將優於 FY26。 
工程業務 2H26 進入工程傳統認列旺季 
大型案件有所斬獲，環保工程在手訂單約近 200 億水平。隨下半年進入工
程傳統認列旺季，研究部預期營收將逐季走高，預估 FY26 全年工程業務營
收將年增 20%以上。崇越持續進行海外布局，目前東南亞案件持續進行
中，亦逐漸朝美國 AI 領域工程拓展，今年將陸續於多國設立據點，奠定工
程業務及半導體材料業務海外發展基礎。 
先進製程擴張為營運成長基礎 
信越於先進製程穩固的市佔率奠定崇越營運成長基礎。受惠先進製程產能持
續釋出且 UTR 維持高檔，加上成熟製程於 2H26 復甦趨勢確立，晶圓代工
產業動能明確。在此背景下，崇越憑藉於光阻劑與矽晶圓的穩固市占，並積
極布局先進封裝材料，奠定產業龍頭地位；此外，工程業務營收認列持續改
善，疊加在手訂單持續維持高水平，挹注營運動能。考量半導體材料業務動
能提升，研究部上調 FY26/FY27 營收獲利預估。

### Page 2

訪談內容 
半導體核心材料需求強勁、環工業務受惠於大型專案認列挹注 2Q26 營收表現 
光阻劑、矽晶圓、光罩等半導體核心材料需求持續強勁，另環保工程業務受惠於大型專案認列，
營收呈現季增，使 2Q26 營收達 208.3 億元(YoY+22.6%/QoQ +12.3%)，創下歷史新高，亦優
於 我 們 先 前 預 期 的 194.1 億元。 產 品 組 合 轉 好帶動 毛利率 持 續 維 持 高 檔 ， 為13.5% 
(YoY+1.8ppts/ QoQ-0.1ppts)，符合我們先前預期的 13.3%。EPS 創下歷史新高，達 7.92 元
(YoY+65.5%/QoQ+15.8%)，優於研究部原先預期的 6.58 元。 
綜觀 7~9 月營收皆維持高檔，研究部推估主要受惠於晶圓代工業產能持續擴張，且另有漲價挹
注營收表現， 3Q26 營收為 236.7 億元 (YoY+34.9%/QoQ+13.6%)；預估毛利率將 持穩於
13.3%(QoQ -0.2ppts/ YoY +1.6ppts) ， 產 品 組 合 維 持 穩 定；預期 EPS 為 8.42 元
(YoY+46.7%/QoQ+6.4%)。

半導體材料業務仍為今明年成長主旋律 
半導體主力產品及新品需求皆見成長，帶動 半導體材料業務持續維持季增。 光阻劑業務營收於
1H26 年增率達 30%以上，研究部上調 FY26 光阻劑營收全年年增率達 30%以上。矽晶圓市場需
求情況逐漸轉好，受惠於先進製程及記憶體廠 UTR 平均達 95~100%，出貨價格持續依 LTA 進度
調整。除光阻劑、矽晶圓等隨先進製程產能開出，藍寶石基板、散熱材料及 Underfill 等新品亦開
始於 1H26 成長力道逐漸顯著，使其他電子材料 1H26 年增率達 30%以上。除需求成長外，在油
價持續 維持高檔下，日本信越開始針對部分產品進行漲價，依據產品類別不同，漲價幅度約
15~20%，其中例如 FOSB 受成本影響較大且需求高，售價可能提升將更為顯著，研究部預期半
導體材料產品調漲效應將延續至 4Q26，帶動公司 FY26 整體營運逐季走高，FY27 營運表現亦將
優於 FY26。

工程業務 2H26 進入工程傳統認列旺季 
大型案件有所斬獲，環保工程在手訂單約近 200 億水平，分布於台灣、中國、新加坡、越南、馬來
西亞各區域。2Q26 工程業務營收顯著回穩，顯示在手訂單已逐步轉化為實際營收。隨下半年進入
工程傳統認列旺季 ， 研究部預期營收將逐季走高 ， 預估FY26 全年工程業務營收將年增 20%以上 。
崇越持續進行海外布局，目前東南亞案件持續進行中，亦逐漸朝美國 AI 領域工程拓展，今年將陸
續於多國設立據點，奠定工程業務及半導體材料業務海外發展基礎。

### Page 3

先進製程擴張為營運成長基礎 
信越於先進製程穩固的市佔率奠定崇越營運成長基礎。受惠先進製程產能持續釋出且 UTR 維持
高檔，加上成熟製程於 2H26 復甦趨勢確立，晶圓代工產業動能明確。在此背景下，崇越憑藉於
光阻劑與矽晶圓的穩固市占，並積極布局先進封裝材料，奠定產業龍頭地位；此外，工程業務營
收認列持續改善，疊加在手訂單持續維持高水平，挹注營運動能。考量半導體材料業務動能提升 ，
研究部上調 FY26/FY27 營 收 獲 利 預 估 ， 預 估FY26/27 營收分別為 865.0/964.7 億元
(YoY+28.0%/11.5%)，毛利率分別達 13.4%/13.3%，FY26/FY27 EPS 分別為 30.72/32.34 元
(YoY+42.6%/5.3%)。評價方面，崇越目前交易於 19.4/18.4 倍之今明年獲利預估，研究部維持
買進投資建議，目標價調升至 700 元，以 21x FY27 獲利預估。

公司簡介 
崇越科技成立於 1990 年，為半導體製程材料代理通路商，主要業務包含 1)半導體相關材料：在
中國及台灣獨家代理日廠信越化學(4063 JP)的矽晶圓/光阻劑/其他製程材料 ， 崇越與信越合作關
係緊密，先後合資成立信越光電、台灣信越半導體股份有限公司、崇越石英等公司。另外崇越亦
代理 FUJIMI(5384 JP)的研磨液。2) 光電相關材料：包含LED/LCD/其他電子材料等。3) 環保工
程：以廠務廢水處理為主，另有機電工程、水回收等廠務工程，主要來自蘇州工程及台灣建越。
4) 綠色能源：太陽能系統工程整合服務等。 
從客戶面來看，半導體相關材料業務中，台積電(2330 TT)為其最大客戶，占比營收約 20%，另
有如中芯國際、聯電、美光等客戶。環保工程業務客戶則有記憶體、面板及其他電子下游產業。

### Page 4

ESG 政策 
崇越在 MSCI ESG 評鑑 BB，台灣永續評鑑中評級為 AA，公司治理評鑑前 20％。另外崇越為富
邦公司治理 ETF(00692)成分股，其中於「經濟面」表現尤其突出，特別是股東權益、董事會結構
與運作、風險及危機管理。 
 利害關係人溝通與重大議題管理 
崇越科技重視與利害關係人溝通 ， 日常各項業務均保持與利害關係人良好溝通與互動 ， 依循2021
年改版之 GRI 通用準則 ， 依永續性脈絡 、 重大性 、 完整性及利害關係人包容性 ， 進行鑑別 、 排序 、
確認與檢視等之重大議題管理流程。 
 風險管理 
崇越科技考量更多異常事件應變措施 ， 提早整合公司發展政策 ， 可於危機發生時 ， 成為存續企業 。
成立資訊安全委員會，掌管營運所需之資訊科技相關事項。此外，2023 年亦成立資安室，設置
資訊安全專責主管與資安人員 ， 負責資訊安全相關工作與落實 。 已規劃於2024 年導入 ISO27001
資訊安全管理系統，預計 2025 取證認證。

風險管理式升息而非緊縮週期，景氣成長趨勢不變
2資料來源：高盛，中國信託商業銀行整理，2026/9/17
◼ 貨幣政策與殖利率： 基準情境認為年底前再升
息一碼後，2027上半年進入按兵不動的觀察期 ，
2027年下半年有望轉向寬鬆立場 。推演殖利率
4Q26偏高表現、1H27適度回落。
◼ 投資建議： 股市估值有壓不表示趨勢路徑改變 ，
上半年資金雨露均霑的普漲行情可能逐漸降溫 ，
但獲利能見度高 、基本面持續改善的族群 ，仍
可望獲得資金青睞並展現相對優勢。
熱門議題/資產影響推估 市場焦點
➢ 風險管理式升息而非緊縮週期： 9月FOMC會議全
票通過升息一碼至 3.75%-4.0%，這是自2023年7
月以來的首次升息 。預料2026年底再升息一碼 ，
其後進入觀察期。
➢ 殖利率4Q26偏高表現 、1H27適度回落 ，股市短
線估值有壓 ：美債殖利率通常要等確認升息到頂
後才會回落 ，市場往往在升息前因政策不確定性
而較為震盪；然而 ，一旦後續經濟數據確認景氣
仍處於擴張軌道 ，即使估值面受到利率壓抑 ，企

業獲利成長仍有望成為推動股市續漲的主要動能 。
升息前後的股市震盪不影響趨勢

聯準會升息落地，股債市未見明顯衝擊
全球投資級債因利率上升而承壓，非投資級債穩健
盤勢回顧
股市情緒受到影響但未失控
3
• 升息預期推高公債及信用債殖利率，但升息落地後有趨穩現象：早在聯準會利率會議召開前夕，市場不斷出現
升息聲浪，推高10年期公債利率一度站上5%，壓抑9月以來信用債市表現，尤其以投資級債表現最為弱勢，也
拖累全年總報酬率轉負 ，但利差走勢其實相當平穩 ，８月以來美投資級債利差大致在 75~85基點區間震盪 。
FOMC會議後公債利率略自高檔回落，並沒有因為升息而持續創高，信用債市殖利率也趨於穩定。
• 股市情緒受到影響但未失控 ：9月以來股市情緒受到一定程度影響 ，不過並未出現恐慌情緒 ，僅利率敏感性類
股震盪較大，非核心消費之外的類股年至今仍多收正報酬。聯準會升息落地後，股市單日跌幅也可控。
資料來源：左， Bloomberg ，2026/9/17，右，Bloomberg，2026/9/17。中國信託商業銀行整理，2026/9/17

### Page 4

©  CTBC
風險管理式升息，而非典型的升息循環重新啟動
2026年底前預期還有一次升息
貨幣政策
通膨廣度其實已有所收斂，預期此一趨勢將延續
4
• 風險管理式升息 ，而非典型的升息循環重新啟動 ：9月FOMC會議全票通過升息一碼至 3.75%-4.0%，這是自
2023年7月以來的首次升息。本次會議的重點可歸納如下：(1)利率預估點陣圖顯示至2026年底前還有一碼的升息
空間；(2)上調通膨與經濟成長預估值，同時下調失業率，是一個很鷹派的組合，不過調整幅度其實很小，2026
年的整體PCE通膨和核心PCE通膨僅上調0.1個百分點；(3)判斷本次會議並非典型的升息循環重新啟動，而是「不
承諾路徑的風險管理式升息」。
• 年底前再升息一碼後進入觀察期 ：搭配利率預估點陣圖，以及本行對2027年通膨走勢的判斷(8月的CPI數據已
經看到通膨廣度已經有收斂態勢，搭配本次升息，預期此一趨勢將延續，2Q27通膨有望降至2.5%以下)，本行
基準情境認為年底前再升息一碼後，2027上半年進入按兵不動的觀察期，2027年下半年有望轉向寬鬆立場。

資料來源：左， Fed ，2026/9/17，右，Robin Brooks，2026/9。中國信託商業銀行整理，2026/9
CPI和PCE通膨中的類別，月增率(年化)高於3%的比重

### Page 5

推演殖利率4Q26偏高表現、1H27適度回落
市場初步反應: 長率未進一步上揚
美債利率
美債殖利率通常要等確認升息到頂後才會回落
5
• 聯準會升息證明公信力，美債市場大致正向表現：9月FOMC會議前市場已先定價升息，而使得美國10年期公債
殖利率來到5%以上，而在FOMC會議後短率上揚、長率大致穩定，顯示市場主要重新定價近期政策變動，而非同
步大幅上修長期通膨或長期利率風險，是一項相對正面的訊號。
• 推演殖利率4Q26偏高表現、1H27適度回落：根據歷史經驗，美債殖利率通常要等確認升息到頂後才會回落，
4Q26在鷹派論調+期中選舉+高油價的影響下，研判美國10年期公債殖利率維持偏高水位 。2027年隨通膨放
緩，市場確認停止升息後，美債殖利率將適度回落。預估未來兩季美國10年期公債殖利率的區間將處於4.5%-
5.1%，美國30年期公債殖利率則在 5.1%-5.5%之間。尾端風險是「後續的升息不斷超過市場預期 」，不過在
油價不持續失控向上的情況下，尾端風險的機率並不高。
資料來源：左， Bloomberg ，2026/9/17，右，3FOURTEEN，2026/9。中國信託商業銀行整理，2026/9/17

註: 紅-科技鏈、綠-受戰爭影響、紫-受惠戰爭、黑-其他、藍色-獲利穩健
過去一個月企業獲利預估值變化
AI成長趨勢
受惠戰爭
• 景氣擴張週期尚未結束 ，升息對股市影響有限 ：9月FOMC決議升息一碼，但股市反應相對有限 。一方面，市
場已提前消化升息預期；更重要的是，過去一年全球景氣維持穩定擴張，且前景仍偏正向。回顧過往景氣擴張
階段的升息循環，市場往往在升息前因政策不確定性而較為震盪；然而 ，一旦後續經濟數據確認景氣仍處於擴
張軌道，即使估值面受到利率壓抑，企業獲利成長仍有望成為推動股市續漲的主要動能。
• 估值壓縮，選股回到獲利能見度：在股市策略方面，現階段獲利成長動能最強的仍是受惠於 AI長期趨勢的科技
供應鏈。部分科技股已於7至8月率先修正整理，不僅評價壓力有所緩解，也提供較具吸引力的布局機會。不過，
中東地緣衝突尚未落幕，加上國際油價維持高檔，使國防、能源等受惠地緣局勢的族群，成為兼具主題性與避
險特性的配置選擇。整體而言，在聯準會重啟升息的環境下，上半年資金雨露均霑的普漲行情可能逐漸降溫，

但獲利能見度高、基本面持續改善的族群，仍可望獲得資金青睞並展現相對優勢。

• 基本面穩健態勢不變，有助穩定信用債利差：FOMC會議證實美國經濟韌性，在景氣成長及企業獲利正向循環
未結束前，穩健債信將支持信用債利差走勢持穩，雖然目前美國公債利率已大致反應升息預期，但政策不確定
性仍牽動短線較大波動，利率敏感度較低的非投資級債預期將有相對突出的表現 ，而高利率及高債信品質的族
群則可長期持有以息收為主，而外幣債與美國雙政策連動性較低，新當債與澳幣債走勢可望回歸自身貨幣政策。

支撐，但此波強勢更多來自地緣政治與高利率環境 ，而非美國基本面出現結構性轉強 。Fed本次升息後美元指
數當日上升約0.6%，美元指數再度回測100。從聯準會官員利率點陣圖判斷，短期利率路徑較先前預期偏高，

支撐美元表現；惟在通膨回穩後 ，貨幣政策仍有機會重新轉向較寬鬆立場 。留意4Q26共和黨失去絕對多數後
對財政樽節的預期，美元短線路徑轉強。美元指數目標價從97上修至99。

Document 461: イベントカレンダー『各国の主要政治・経済イベント予定』(2026年11月号)

Source PDF: all report pdf/イベントカレンダー『各国の主要政治・経済イベント予定』(2026年11月号).pdf
Report source: DLRI
Date: 2026-10-20
Pages: 1
Status: ok

### Page 1

各国の主要政治 ・ 経済イベン ト予定 （10月 ７ 日時点）
11月の各国主要政治 ・ 経済イベン ト
日本 米国 グローバル
上旬
中間選挙 （3日）
10月ISM製造業景況指数 （3日）
10月ISM非製造業景況指数 （5日）

（豪） 金融政策決定会合 （3日）
（英） 金融政策決定会合 （5日）

7-9月期GDP1次速報(16日)
10月貿易統計 （18日）
10月小売売上高 （17日) (欧） 7-9月期GDP改定値 （13日）
下旬
10月消費者物価指数 （20日）
10月鉱工業指数 （30日）
7-9月期GDP改定値 （25日）
（注）  作成時点で、 第一ライフ資産運用経済研究所経済調査部が信ずるに足ると判断した情報に基づき作成していますが、 その正確性、 完全性に対す
る責任は負いません。
米国中間選挙は、 大統領任期4年の中間である2年目に行われる連邦議会選挙です。 2026年11月3日に実施され、 下

院の全435議席と上院の約3分の1が改選対象となります。 政権に対する 「中間評価」 と しての意味合いを持ち、 今後の
政策運営に大きな影響を与えます。
本選に先立って行われた各党の候補者選び （予備選挙） では、 トランプ大統領の後押しを受けた共和党候補が相次い
で勝ち上がった一方、 民主党でも現職政権への対決姿勢を鮮明にする候補が選ばれるなど、 両党ともに熱心な支持層
の意向が強く出た結果となりました。 しかし本選に向けては、 高止まりする物価や生活費負担への不満を背景に トランプ
大統領の支持率が低下傾向にあると報じ られるなど、 政権側には逆風も吹いています。 有権者の間では、 長引く中東情
勢の悪化を受けたインフレや雇用環境といった 「生活防衛」 が最大の投票判断基準となっているほか、 国境管理 ・ 不法移
民問題や人工妊娠中絶の権利といった社会的テーマも、 勝敗の鍵を握る激戦区 （スイング ・ ステー ト） での浮動票の行方
を左右する重要な争点です。
執筆時点の情勢と しては、 上院は共和党優位、 下院は接戦ながら民主党がやや有利とみられています。 先行きへの影

響と しては、 共和党が上下両院の過半数を維持する 「 ト リプルレ ッ ド」 が続けば、 トランプ政権の政策実行力が高まり規制
緩和などが進みやすくなる一方、 関税引き上げや財政悪化への懸念が残ります。 反対に、 民主党が下院を制して 「ねじれ
議会」 となった場合は、 法案や予算が通りにく くなり政権運営が停滞する反面、 過度な強硬策にブレーキがかかる点がか
えって安心材料と受け止められる側面もあります。 中間選挙の結果は、 米国の国内政治にとどまらず、 今後の通商政策
や金利の先行きを見極める分岐点となります。 （主席エコノミス ト ：  阿原健一郎）
11月の政治 ・ 経済イベン ト 「米国中間選挙」

Document 467: インド消費者物価（26年９月）～総合ＣＰＩは5.5％に上昇、食品高が一段と加速

Source PDF: all report pdf/インド消費者物価（26年９月）～総合ＣＰＩは5.5％に上昇、食品高が一段と加速.pdf
Report source: NLI
Date: 2026-10-13
Pages: 3
Status: ok

### Page 1

１．総合ＣＰＩは5.5％に上昇、農村部では6％台へ 
インド統計・計画実施省が10月12日に公表した2026年9月の消費者物価指数（以下、ＣＰＩ）に
よると、総合ＣＰＩ上昇率は前年同月比5.5％となり、前月の4.8％から上昇した（図表１）。総合
インフレ率はインド準備銀行（ＲＢＩ）の物価目標である4％を4カ月連続で上回り、許容レンジの
上限である6％に近づいた。 
地域別にみると、都市部では前年同月比4.9％（前月：4.3％）、農村部では同6.1％（同5.2％）
となり、 いずれも前月から上昇した。 とりわけ農村部ではインフレ率がＲＢＩの許容レンジの上限
である6％を上回り、都市部との差も前月の0.9％ポイントから1.2％ポイントに拡大した。 
９月のＣＰＩは、野菜・豆類を中心に食品価格の上昇が加速したほか、外食・宿泊サービスや交
通など幅広い分野で伸びが高まっており、 物価上昇圧力が一段と強まったことを示す内容となった。
一方、貴金属価格の影響が大きい身の回り品・その他の伸びは鈍化し、保健医療など一部のサービ
ス価格はなお低い伸びにとどまっている。 今後は、 食品や輸送コストの上昇が幅広い品目に波及し、
基調的なインフレ圧力をさらに強めるかが焦点となろう。

２．食品インフレは7％台後半に加速、野菜・豆類が急上昇 
食品・飲料の上昇率は前年同月比7.4％となり、前月の同5.7％から大きく加速した（図表１）。
このうち消費者食品価格指数（ＣＦＰＩ）は同7.7％となり、前月の同6.0％から上昇した。 
食品の主要項目別にみると、砂糖・菓子類が前年同月比13.8％、果物が同10.8％、調理食品が同
10.0％と二桁の高い伸びを示したほか、野菜・豆類が同9.2％、肉類が同8.7％、油脂が同8.3％、

### Page 2

３．燃料・光熱費は伸び拡大、原油価格は再び上昇 
燃料・光熱費の上昇率は前年同月比 3.2％となり、
前月の同3.1％からやや上昇した（図表１）。エネル
ギー価格の上昇率はなお比較的低いものの、これ
まで総合ＣＰＩを抑制してきた効果は弱まりつつあ
る。 
インド原油バスケット価格は、２月末の中東情勢
の緊迫化を受けて３月に急騰した後、いったん低
下したものの、７月以降は米国とイランの対立再燃
などを背景に再び上昇し、直近では１バレル＝120
ドル台の高水準に達している（図表３）。為替相場も
26年央にかけてルピー安が進んだ後、足元では１ドル＝95ルピー前後の安値圏で推移しており、原油高
が続けば、輸入コストを通じて国内の燃料価格に上昇圧力が及ぶ可能性がある。 
一方、輸送関連では高めの伸びが続いている。交通全体の上昇率は前年同月比5.0％となり、前月の同
4.6％から上昇した。内訳をみると、自家用車などの維持・利用費は同7.5％、貨物輸送サービスは同
14.7％と高い伸びを示したほか、旅客輸送サービスも同3.8％と前月の同3.4％から上昇した。一方、輸送
機器の購入は同▲2.6％と下落が続いた。今後、原油高が長期化すれば、燃料価格だけでなく、物流費な
どを通じて食品やサービス価格にも上昇圧力が波及する可能性がある。

４．コアインフレは4.3％に上昇、物価上昇の裾野が広がる 
食品・飲料および燃料・光熱費を除くコアイ
ンフレは、前年同月比4.3％となり、前月の同
4.2％から小幅に上昇した（図表１）。 
分野別にみると、身の回り品・その他の財・
サービスや外食・宿泊サービスが引き続き高い
伸びを示した（図表４）。このほか、交通は前
年同月比5.0％（前月：4.6％）、衣料・履物は
同3.9％ （同3.6％） 、 教育サービスは同3.9％ （同
3.7％） 、 情報通信は同2.4％ （同2.0％） 、 娯楽・
スポーツ・文化は同2.2％（同1.7％）と、いずれも前月から伸びが高まった。一方、保健医療は同
1.4％と低い伸びにとどまった。

### Page 3

５．ＲＢＩは10月に利上げ、物価上振れリスクへの警戒を強める 
９月は総合ＣＰＩ、食品・飲料、コアＣＰＩ
の上昇率が揃って高まり、物価上昇圧力は前月
より一段と強まった。先行きは、降雨不足が食
品価格に及ぼす影響に加え、原油高によるコス
ト上昇が幅広い品目に波及するか が焦点となる。 
食品価格の先行きを左右する南西モンスーン
の降雨量は、６～９月の累計で平年の約87％に
とどまった。一方、カリフ作物の作付面積は10
月２日時点で約 1.12 億ヘクタールとなり、 平年
を約１％上回ったものの、前年同期を約１％下回った。作付けはおおむね平年水準を確保したが、
降雨不足や地域的なばらつきが収穫量に及ぼす影響に加え、 今後の冬作物の作付けへの影響にも注
意が必要である。 
こうしたなか、 ＲＢＩは10 月７日の金融政策決定会合で政策金利を 0.25％引き上げて 5.50％と
し、金融政策スタンスを「中立」から「調整された引き締め」に変更した。ＲＢＩは降雨不足や原
油高、 物価上昇圧力の広がりを警戒し、2026/27 年度のＣＰＩ上昇率予測を 5.0％から 5.2％に引き
上げた。 ９月のインフレ率上昇により、利上げ後の政策金利との差でみた実質金利はほぼゼロ％と
なった（図表５） 。 
もっとも、足元の物価上昇には食品や原油など供給面の要因が大きく、 基調的な需要インフレが
全面的に強まっているとは言い切れない。 ＲＢＩは10～12 月期のＣＰＩ上昇率を 6.0％と予測して
おり、当面は物価上昇圧力の広がりやコスト上昇の二次的な波及を慎重に見極めるとみられる。 食
品インフレの高止まりや原油高が続き、コアインフレへの波及が一段と明確になれば、 追加利上げ
観測が強まる可能性がある。
